Abstract: We derive a microscopic relation between the many-body Chern number $C$ of the fermionic ground state and the braiding of fluxes in $\mathbb{Z}_{2}$ gauge theory coupled to matter: under a spectral gap condition, dressed flux loops obey $W_2W_1=e^{i\fracπ{2}C}W_1W_2$ on the ground-state sector. We realize $C=1$ by gauging charge parity in a weakly interacting Qi-Wu-Zhang insulator. Reflection positivity and chessboard estimates show that flux defects are massive, which yields a gapped, topologically ordered phase with four locally indistinguishable ground states.

Abstract: The Poisson $σ$-model (PSM) over a closed worldsheet $Σ$ is reinterpreted as a theory of connections on a trivial principaloid bundle $P_0 = Σ\times G \to Σ$ with the symplectic structure groupoid $(G,ω)$ integrating the Poisson target $(M,Π)$, and that of sections of the shadow bundle $F_0 = Σ\times M \to Σ$ - as defined in 2503.09886 [math.DG]. The flat directions of the PSM are explained as linearisations of automorphisms of $P_0$, modelled on maps from $Σ$ into the group of lagrangean bisections of $G$, which induce Poisson automorphisms of $(M,Π)$. The gauge anomaly of the ensuing field theory is derived and demonstrated to vanish for prequantisable $ω$. A general anomaly-cancellation mechanism is proposed, which couples the bare model - rewritten as the holonomy of a connection-dependent trivial gerbe on $F_0$ - to a background gerbe over $M$ endowed with a $G$-equivariant structure. The coupling takes the form of the tensor product of the two gerbes and the extended PSM is defined as the holonomy of the product gerbe. An essential generalisation of the theory is then proposed - to shadows $F$ of principaloid bundles $P$ of arbitrary topology, in keeping with the interpretation of the gauging as descent to the orbistack $M//G$. The generalisation uses local product gerbes depending on local connections over trivialisations of $F$. The $G$-equivariant structure is proven to ensure a 2-stacky descent of the local gerbes to a global one over $F$. Its holonomy is taken as the definition of the generalised PSM. A cohomological discussion of obstructions against the descent is provided. Finally, a reformulation of the generalised PSM as a field theory with a topological gauge-symmetry defect is given, in which the $G$-equivariant structure is employed - alongside the transition cocycle of $P$ - as the datum of the defect.

</details>

Abstract: The paper presents a brief overview of recent advances in the mathematical theory of evolution equations for quantum many-particle systems.
  The structure of expansions representing non-perturbative solutions to the Cauchy problem for hierarchies of fundamental evolution equations for many quantum particles is outlined. It has been shown that the concept of cumulants of operator semigroups forms the basis for non-perturbative representation of solutions to hierarchies of evolution equations that describe the state and observables.
  It is proven that the constructed dynamics of correlations underlie the description of the evolution of the state, both for finitely and infinitely many particles, governed by the BBGKY hierarchy for reduced density operators or by the hierarchy of nonlinear evolution equations for reduced correlation operators. In fact, it is demonstrated that the behavior of particle correlations governs the dynamics of states in quantum systems with an infinite number of particles.

</details>

Abstract: We construct the canonical boundary algebras of Jackiw--Teitelboim gravity coupled to free bosonic matter. For a nonempty finite or countable family of species with lowest weights greater than one half, the two boundary algebras are mutual commutants and nonhyperfinite type $\mathrm{II}_\infty$ factors. Their nonzero finite corners are nonhyperfinite type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors. The construction uses bounded heat-regularized fields and a Hilbert algebra, which gives a faithful normal semifinite trace without a path-integral argument. The weight restriction is sharp: elementary point fields remain unbounded after positive heat regularization when $0<d\leq1/2$. For one massless scalar, specified bounded observables and normal states have a high energy limit described by a hyperfinite continuous core. We explain why this limit does not preserve the nonhyperfiniteness of the exact algebra.

</details>

Abstract: We show that a Lotka--Volterra system with an interior equilibrium admits a metriplectic structure, with a Volterra function $V_C$ as energy and an entropy variable for the environment, if and only if Volterra's dissipativity condition $CA+A^{\top}C\le0$ holds, and we construct it explicitly with a log-canonical Poisson bivector. Adding noise through the fluctuation--dissipation relation of stochastic GENERIC yields multiplicative environmental noise, with covariance fixed by the symmetric part of $AC^{-1}$, whose stationary law is an explicit Gibbs measure: a product of independent Gamma distributions. Unlike known Gibbs measures for stochastic Lotka--Volterra systems, this holds for nonsymmetric interactions, predator--prey included. The stationary law determines only the equilibrium and $C/k$: every interaction matrix in an open cone of dimension $n^2$, in particular any Poisson part, produces with its compatible noise the same law as uncoupled stochastic logistic equations. Gamma-distributed, uncorrelated abundance fluctuations, as observed in microbial communities, are therefore no evidence of absence of interactions. The interactions are, however, visible in time: the zero-lag derivative of the log-abundance covariance equals $A(C/k)^{-1}$, and its antisymmetric part is the Poisson part, which carries all the stationary entropy production, computed in closed form. Finally, for general noise we prove an exact Lyapunov-type identity $AK+K^\top A^\top+Q=0$ for $K=\mathrm{Cov}(x,\log x)$ at any stationary solution. It implies that the populations are independent if and only if the noise is metriplectically compatible, and gives an explicit lower bound on interspecies cross-covariances in terms of the distance of $Q$ to the compatible family. Simulations of a predator--prey model show that the bound is nearly sharp.

</details>

Abstract: This is the first of two papers devoted to spatial Fréchet structures on AF algebras and their derivations. Here, we study the simplest example of such a structure: the Fréchet algebra of almost-local compact operators associated with a fixed orthonormal basis. We revisit the characterization of its continuous derivations in terms of infinite matrices and their operator-theoretic implementation on rapidly decreasing vectors. Reformulating this description in the language of 0-chains, borrowed from the theory of quantum many-body systems, we establish a sufficient condition for continuity in terms of spatial growth. Finally, we construct a Fréchet-continuous symmetric derivation that does not generate a one-parameter group of automorphisms of this algebra that is strongly continuous with respect to its Fréchet topology.

</details>

Abstract: We construct two-parameter Baxter $\mathbb{Q}$-operators for the transfer matrices of noncompact open $SL(2,\mathbb{C})$ BC-type spin chains with general boundary interactions, treating both one-boundary and two-boundary models. The $\mathbb{Q}$-operators are defined as traces of general monodromy matrices over infinite-dimensional auxiliary spaces carrying principal series representations of $SL(2,\mathbb{C})$.
  At specific values of parameters the two-parameter $\mathbb{Q}$-operators degenerate into simpler one-parameter Baxter operators. We prove that the general transfer matrices factorize into products of these one-parameter $Q$-operators. We provide both an algebraic derivation based on local operator identities and a diagrammatic proof using Feynman diagrammatic techniques.
  Finally, we apply the quantum Separation of Variables (SoV) method to the spectral problem of the one-boundary transfer matrix. We derive the system of separated difference equations for the transfer matrix eigenfunctions and show that, under the assumption of kernel factorization, it reduces to a one-dimensional difference equation coinciding precisely with the Baxter equation for eigenvalues of appropriately normalized one-parameter Baxter $Q$-operator.

</details>

Abstract: We give a probabilistic characterization of the ideals of resolvent algebras. Abstract: Higher-order topological insulators and superconductors, under combined point and fundamental symmetries, display topological modes supported by boundaries of higher codimensions, such as a corner mode. In this work, we explain a theoretical device that enables us to claim that there exists {\it one and only one} intrinsic chiral-symmetric higher-order topological phase for the quarter-plane geometry with point-symmetry given by reflection about the diagonal of the corner. The robust properties of the corner mode are investigated by COMSOL simulations.

Abstract: Data-driven constitutive model discovery is recast as causal discovery of mechanism parent sets using time-unrolled structural causal models. Hard-coding balance laws and constitutive skeletons, candidate graphs score environment--mechanism and state--state edges via weak-form equilibrium gaps evaluated from full-field displacements, avoiding the need to measure stresses or internal variables. An adaptive lasso and a NOTEARS-type constraint ensure graph acyclicity and an explicit evaluation order, treating climate-chamber protocols as interventions. Automatic differentiation yields the score's Gauss--Newton matrix as a local identifiability certificate, driving greedy D-optimal test design. In a synthetic study on a hygro-thermo-viscoelastic damaging plate (8 true edges among 13 candidates), interventional data recover the exact true graph in 7 of 10 runs (0.01px displacement noise) with zero attribution error. Conversely, observational data with a temperature--moisture correlation of $-0.998$ fail entirely because confounded scores are 250--380 times flatter. Observationally discovered models reproduce training climates (3.6% error) but mispredict hot and humid climates by up to 41.5% (16.9% average), whereas interventionally discovered models stay within 3.2%. A correlation sweep shows the certificate successfully predicts attribution errors, though weak-form score noise bias remains a primary limitation. Computed without knowledge of the truth, the D-optimal design selects test sets ranked second or third out of 126 candidates.

</details>

Abstract: We study various families of non-periodic configurations of symbols assigned to vertices of regular graphs. We restrict ourselves to such systems where considered configurations support a uniquely ergodic measure. Symbols can be interpreted as particles and we are concerned with configurations which minimize the interaction energy of particles, the so-called ground-state configurations. One of the main questions then is whether ground-state configurations are stable with respect to small perturbations of interactions. Such stability may be interpreted as a sort of rigidity of non-periodic structures such as tilings, substitution systems, Sturmian systems, and other uniquely ergodic non-periodic systems. We introduce a property of bounded fluctuations of finite patterns of non-periodic configurations, the so-called Strict Boundary Property. We discuss how relevant this property is for stability of non-periodic structures. Examples of Robinson's tilings, Thue-Morse, and Sturmian systems are presented. Some open problems are formulated.

</details>

Abstract: Norton's dome illustrates how a Newtonian initial-value problem can admit both rest and delayed departure from the same initial state. We examine how this nonuniqueness changes when the point particle is replaced by a rigid sphere of positive radius. For a generalized family of Norton's domes, we show that any fixed positive sphere radius prevents spontaneous departure from rest at the apex. We then extend the analysis to more general bodies and establish two sufficient conditions: a geometric constraint on the distance between the center of mass and the apex, and an interior tangent ball condition for rigid bodies whose center of mass is vertically aligned with the initial contact point. Both arguments combine geometric estimates with an energy inequality and Grönwall's inequality. The first condition also applies to passive deformable bodies without releasable internal energy. These results identify explicit conditions under which finite-body geometry excludes the spontaneous motion allowed by Norton's point-particle model.

</details>

Abstract: Following Bach, Lieb and Solovej, quadratic Hamiltonians can be defined as generators of unitary groups implementing one-parameter groups of Bogoliubov transformations. We develop an analogous dynamical definition of generalized spin--boson Hamiltonians. For a single spin level, we review results of Guichardet and Dereziński: the van Hove Hamiltonians, including the renormalized ones, are exactly the generators of affine Bogoliubov dynamics that do not mix creation and annihilation operators. For several spin levels, there is no analogue of this group, and we introduce spin-dressed implementations, that is to say the free flow of the fields is implemented up to bounded operator-valued displacements, which vanish at a rate measured by a so-called regularity scale and have a spin observable, the coupling map, as derivative at time zero. The one-particle Hamiltonian $Υ$ and the coupling map are determined by the Hamiltonian, and the field obeys a quantum Langevin equation. Spin-dressed implementations in Fock space, at the finest scale and for form factors in $D(Υ^{-1/2})$, are exactly the generalized spin--boson Hamiltonians, unique up to the spin Hamiltonian. At the roughest scale, the renormalized spin--boson Hamiltonian of a two-level system with ultraviolet-singular form factor and arbitrary tunnelling is a spin-dressed implementation, and the definition alone yields asymptotic Weyl operators on the test functions along which the coupling map is integrable, a dense set under standard assumptions.

</details>

Abstract: We study quantum Hamiltonians whose separated angular component is expressed through a generic Painlevé VI transcendent. We construct generalized ladder operators for one-dimensional Schrödinger operators with a quadratic spectral variable. For the Painlevé VI angular Hamiltonian these operators satisfy $[N,J_\pm]=\pm2kJ_\pm$, where $N=\sqrt M$, and close through an eighth-degree norm polynomial. Combining the angular shifts with the radial shift operators of the oscillator, Coulomb and free Bessel systems yields higher-order integrals and deformed Racah-type polynomial algebras for the two-dimensional superintegrable models. This construction provides new families of Tremblay-Turbiner-Winternitz (TTW)- and Post-Winternitz (PW)-like Hamiltonians with integrals of arbitrarily high order and Painlevé VI in the potential. This framework also recovers models from the classification of third- and fourth-order integrals as particular cases. For a representative oscillator extension we compute the Casimir explicitly and recover the finite-dimensional energy spectrum from a deformed-oscillator realization. We then analyze the special-solution sector using the explicit families collected by Gromak, Laine and Shimomura. The resulting potentials are organized into a small number of spectral classes, and the distinction between formal zeros of the ladder norm and physical self-adjoint representations is made explicit. The regular nonclassical rational examples are rational Darboux deformations of the trigonometric Pöschl--Teller system and lead to strictly isospectral exceptional-Jacobi sectors or to state-adding representations with an isolated angular level. A singular branch produces an interior inverse-square barrier and a split-domain problem. For one regular exceptional sector and two state-adding sectors we display the polynomial algebras.

</details>

Abstract: The two-dimensional one-component plasma is a system of point charges that repel each other logarithmically, in a uniform neutralizing background. When the inverse temperature $β$ is at most $2$, we prove that its correlations decay exponentially, and that its Gibbs state is unique among point processes whose fluctuations are controlled. This constitutes part of the fluid phase predicted by physicists. As a consequence, we obtain the existence of an infinite-volume limit, extending the Ginibre point process, which we call $\mathrm{Gin}_β$. We show that its truncated two-point function, at the scale of the Debye length, is given to first order in $β$ by the Debye-Hückel approximation.

</details>

A lower bound on its disordered susceptibility excludes the usual Kosterlitz--Thouless law.

Abstract: Recently, a version of U(1) lattice gauge theory on the cubic lattice with a monopole enhancement term was considered in the literature. In this work, we further our understanding of this theory with a mixture of analytical and numerical methods. We show that for sufficiently large monopole enhancement, the monopoles are no longer dilute and they arrange themselves into an antiferromagnetic, Néel-ordered ground state. Nonetheless, a variant of Polyakov's argument for the dilute excitations over the dense ground state leads to an effective sine-Gordon model which is qualitatively similar to that of the standard theory. We also predict analytically the presence of first-order transitions as the monopole chemical potential is varied at fixed gauge coupling, corresponding to a change in the dominant monopole charge. Similarly, we predict a transition in the Ising universality class as the gauge coupling is varied. We then use numerical methods to study the phase diagram of the theory and check some of our predictions.

</details>

Abstract: Quantum electrodynamics in two spacetime dimensions, also known as the Schwinger model, admits a dual formulation in terms of a non-compact boson field. Using a Hamiltonian lattice discretization of the bosonized model, we compute the critical mass of the continuum Schwinger model at $θ=π$. We numerically obtain the ground state on an infinite chain by the variational uniform matrix product state (VUMPS) algorithm, preserving charge conjugation exactly. As a truncated basis for the local Hilbert space, we employ the oscillator basis that diagonalizes the one-site reduced density matrix of the free limit. We constrain the fitting functions for the continuum extrapolation using the Symanzik effective theory for lattice corrections. Our result for the critical mass, $(m/g)_c = 0.333\,555(5)$, agrees with recent staggered-fermion determinations at comparable precision.

</details>

Abstract: We apply the recently developed Symanzik effective Hamiltonian framework to Wilson twisted mass fermions. The effective Hamiltonian is constructed solely based on the symmetries of the lattice Hamiltonian without recourse to the Symanzik effective action in Euclidean space-time. We use it to prove automatic $O(a)$ improvement at maximal twist, as well as $O(a)$ improvement via so-called mass and Wilson average. Moreover, we derive the structure of $O(a)$ counterterms pre-requisite for clover on-shell improvement of Wilson fermions. We show how to remove on-shell redundant contributions using canonical transformations, and find that the well known clover term in isotropic Euclidean space time splits into two terms with independent coefficients.

Abstract: We test numerically a recently proposed prescription for coupling continuum chiral fermions to interpolated lattice gauge fields, in which the fermion weight has a Pauli-Villars (PV) regularized magnitude and a phase given by an Atiyah-Patodi-Singer (APS) $η$-invariant in one higher dimension. We use the anomaly-free two-dimensional $U(1)$ theory with left-handed charges $3,4$ and right-handed charges $5,0$, whose torus determinant is known exactly. At zero magnetic flux, with gauge fields interpolated from lattice links, the Polyakov loops evaluated on a $16\times16$ grid of flat connections agree with the exact values within $0.04\%$, whereas omitting the APS phase substantially reduces them to about $3\%$ of the exact result. The vacuum-polarization response yields an induced mass $m/e=2.82089$, close to $5/\sqrtπ=2.82095$. At unit flux, where zero modes make the determinant vanish, we calculate a twelve-point correlator on a cylinder with the generalized APS boundary condition. For three smooth backgrounds its magnitude agrees with the exact result within $0.8\%$. Without the $η$-invariant its phase deviates by $1.8$-$3.0$ mrad, and with it by $0.2$-$0.5$ mrad. The phase of each charged fermion changes by $15$-$46$ mrad when the gauge field is extended differently into the third dimension, but the change cancels to below $0.2$ mrad in the anomaly-free combination.

Together, these results establish a general framework for understanding how RF-driven DW oscillations are rectified into directed motion, providing new opportunities for their high-frequency control.

Abstract: Phonon hydrodynamics enables collective heat flow beyond the classical diffusive limit and offers a promising route to controlling thermal transport in low-dimensional materials and nanoscale devices. However, quantitative criteria for determining when this hydrodynamic state emerges and where its boundaries lie remain a longstanding challenge in condensed matter physics. Here, we show that the onset of phonon hydrodynamics can be formulated as a non-Hermitian dynamical transition that converts microscopic phonon scattering properties into predictive boundaries for wavelike heat transport. Starting from the Peierls-Boltzmann transport equation, we derive an effective Schrodinger equation for the coupled dynamics of temperature and phonon drift fields, allowing the hydrodynamic regime to be identified directly from material transport properties. Within this framework, a pair of exceptional points quantitatively define the lower and upper boundaries of the hydrodynamic window, providing a critical design criterion beyond the qualitative scattering-rate inequalities proposed by Guyer and Krumhansl six decades ago. Furthermore, we derive an analytical criterion for the existence of hydrodynamic transport, showing that a finite hydrodynamic window can be supported only when the effective thermal diffusivity exceeds the phononic momentum diffusivity. Using ab initio phonon properties as inputs, our theory accurately captures the experimentally observed temperature- and length-dependent ultrafast second-sound dynamics. Abstract: Quantum scattering is a fundamental probe of the scattering region and the quantum dynamics of outgoing particles. In two terminals, the statistics of non-interacting fermions is described by a generalized binomial distribution, that arises from the Pauli exclusion principle, while deviations are a signature of interactions. In this work, we extend this result to a scattering region coupled to three outgoing terminals (or channels), obtaining exact bounds for the statistics of the outgoing fermions. In particular, for two fermions and three terminals, four inequalities involving six measured probabilities determine whether a non-interacting scatterer can explain the observed statistics. Applying these inequalities to recent scattering experiments in cold atoms, we are able to identify a regime where interactions have a significant contribution on the outgoing particles. Abstract: The central limit theorem is among the most fundamental principles in the natural sciences, governing the fluctuations of sums of weakly correlated random variables through Gaussian statistics. At critical points, however, long-range correlations invalidate this paradigm, raising a fundamental question: does any general limit theorem survive at criticality? Although scale invariance has long been recognised as the defining feature of typical critical fluctuations, the behaviour of rare fluctuations has remained largely unexplored, as their extraordinarily small probabilities place them beyond the reach of conventional numerical methods. Recent theoretical advances suggest that universal asymptotic laws {may govern large deviations in systems at criticality}, yet direct numerical evidence has been lacking. Here we provide the first such evidence in the two-dimensional Ising universality class. Using large-scale Tethered Monte Carlo simulations, we probe order-parameter fluctuations with probabilities as small as $10^{-200}$ and show that, once normalised by their characteristic width, the far tails collapse onto a single asymptotic law shared by distinct microscopic models in the same universality class. This collapse is governed by a universal amplitude, whose numerical extraction is compatible with the conjectured exact result we derive from conformal field theory, and points towards a generalised central limit theorem for critical fluctuations.

</details>

Abstract: Driving between nonequilibrium steady states incurs two thermodynamic costs: excess entropy production (EP) resulting from changing control parameters, and housekeeping EP required to sustain steady currents. Conventional fixed-duration optimization minimizes the former while neglecting the latter. Here, we instead optimize excess EP over both the protocol and its duration subject to a fixed leading order of housekeeping-EP budget, thereby minimizing total EP for that budget constraint. In the slow-driving regime, accumulated housekeeping EP acts as an entropic time, mapping the problem to a geodesic optimization under a friction metric weighted by the steady-state EP rate. This resource-based geometry determines optimal paths and schedules for systems that continue to dissipate even in steady states, establishing an intrinsic trade-off between housekeeping and excess EPs. Near equilibrium, this framework further yields geometric lower bounds on total EP, attained with equipartition at the optimal budget given by a modified thermodynamic length. Numerical simulations of a three-state model for an ATP-driven calcium pump reveal distinct optimal protocols compared to fixed-duration driving, confirming the total-EP bounds.

Abstract: The quasiparticle picture provides a quantitative description of entanglement spreading after quantum quenches in integrable systems. In interacting models, however, it relies on the assumption that the quench creates entangled pairs of quasiparticles, which is justified only for the so-called integrable initial states. Here we go beyond this setting by studying quenches in the XXZ spin chain from the $n$-rainbow states, a family of macroscopically homogeneous states formed by increasingly long-range Bell pairs, which are not integrable for $n>1$. The local stationary state after the quench approaches the infinite-temperature state as $n\to\infty$, with corrections of order $1/n$, as we show analytically. Nevertheless, in the noninteracting XX chain the quench produces entangled multiplets of $2n$ species of quasiparticles. Although interspecies correlations vanish as $1/n$, they strongly suppress the growth of the entanglement entropy, whose rate vanishes as $n\to\infty$. We capture this behaviour within a generalized quasiparticle picture. In the interacting XXZ chain, instead, time-dependent density matrix renormalization group (tDMRG) simulations show that the entanglement entropy grows at the rate at which thermodynamic entropy is exchanged across the cut. Within the time scales accessible to tDMRG, the data show no trace of the multiplet suppression. This holds both after homogeneous quenches and after joining the $n$-rainbow state with an infinite-temperature state, for which we conjecture a formula for the von Neumann entropy.

</details>

Abstract: Passive probes in active baths can undergo spontaneous motion driven by nonequilibrium forces that depend on probe velocity and interaction geometry. Using trajectory-response relations that express the bath response to a probe in terms of free-particle displacement statistics, we investigate the dynamics of freely moving probes weakly coupled to ideal active baths. The mean force exerted by the bath defines a velocity-space potential whose minima describe the stable states. By analyzing how the mean force depends on the probe velocity, we identify stable resting and moving states and determine the parameter regime in which they coexist. For elliptical interactions, the resting state becomes unstable first along the narrow axis, resulting in direction selection already at the linear-response level. For fourfold interactions, linear friction remains isotropic, while the nonlinear dependence of the mean force on probe velocity selects axial or diagonal motion and produces directional multistability. We further show that the trajectory-response relations extend to higher orders in the probe strength, where multi-interval free-particle trajectory statistics become relevant. For a Gaussian probe, these higher-order terms shift the speed of the stable moving state.

For the first moment, we establish an exact duality, within MFT, to integrated-current statistics for a deterministic flat initial density. For both $n=1$ and $n=2$, the large-moment tails are dominated by a single adiabatically evolving doublon: a rapidly propagating pair of narrow solitary pulses of the energy density and of the gradient of the conjugate field. The small-second-moment tail is associated with concentration of the energy near the origin at time $T$. Numerical solutions obtained by direct minimization of the MFT action establish the second-order character of this transition and support our analytical predictions. Abstract: We study the recovery of a rank-one signal embedded in a symmetric Euclidean random matrix, whose dense and geometrically correlated entries generally lack rotational invariance. We show that signal recovery through the leading eigenvector is governed by a competition between the principal directions of the noise and the signal. In particular, the signal may already be encoded in a subleading eigendirection, while remaining undetected by the leading principal component. As the signal strength increases, a crossing between competing eigenvalues can change their ordering, causing the leading eigenvector to switch abruptly from a noise-dominated to a signal-bearing direction. This mechanism gives rise to a discontinuous BBP-type transition, in which the overlap with the signal jumps from zero to a finite value. Using the replica method, we reduce the problem to a Fredholm eigenvalue equation and derive general criteria for the existence of a positive recovery threshold, expressed in terms of the unperturbed spectral edge, the spike mean and the spatial averages of the principal eigenfunctions. We obtain analytically the limiting largest eigenvalue, eigenvector-component distribution and signal overlap, and illustrate the results with explicit solutions for quadratic distance and rank-two kernels, finding excellent agreement with numerical diagonalisation.

Abstract: When a system is driven by a prescribed protocol, the entropy production can be decomposed into the housekeeping and non-autonomous contributions, each satisfying a fluctuation theorem. Feedback cooling thus corresponds to heat conduction from the physical bath through the particle to this colder bath, and the information flow can be interpreted as the entropy associated with the heat conduction between $x$ and $D$. We verify these results by numerical simulations.

Abstract: We introduce a continuous-time random walk process via a structured density by tracking the state of the particle as the system evolves. Whenever the particle makes a jump, its internal state either increases by one or resets to zero, so that the internal state evolves according to a renewal process. A wide range of governing integro-differential equations may be obtained from this formulation, some of which exhibit various types of anomalous behaviour. For certain choices of reset probabilities, waiting times and jump distributions, we explicitly derive the mode-dependent memory kernels and investigate their continuum limits. The framework can recover the time-and-space-fractional Fokker-Planck equation, which traditionally models the interplay between long wait times and large jumps and exhibits anomalous diffusion. Furthermore, we obtain a process which converges toward the equilibrium distribution as time approaches some finite value through a sequence of waiting times which decrease geometrically. Finally, we investigate a process that incorporates self-excitation and self-inhibition into the waiting times for each state to produce a mean square displacement proportional to $t^ν$ for any $ν> 0$. Through these examples, we illustrate the link between the microscopic mechanisms in these processes and their non-local macroscopic governing equations.

Abstract: We investigate the transient dynamics of a one-dimensional ballistic random walker under threshold resetting (TR). The walker starts at $x_0>0$, moves towards a threshold at the origin with a velocity drawn from a distribution $φ(v)$, and on reaching the threshold is reset to $x_0$ with a new sampled velocity. Whenever the mean first-passage time to the threshold is finite, the walker reaches a steady state that is uniform on $[0,x_0]$ and independent of $φ(v)$. The relaxation towards it, in contrast, is highly sensitive to the behavior of $φ(v)$. For a large class of $φ(v)$-s, we find, rather remarkably, that the relaxation rate depends explicitly on the spatial coordinate, in contrast to conventional relaxation phenomena, where the rate is typically position-independent. Furthermore, this space-dependent relaxation rate eventually freezes to a constant, position-independent value beyond a critical position, a phenomenon we identify as \textit{freezing transition}. Additionally, for a power-law velocity distribution, the system exhibits an anomalously slow, algebraic relaxation towards the steady state. We also showed that a sharp cutoff in $φ(v)$ generates a shock-like discontinuity in the particle propagator that travels towards the threshold, with a jump height that decays with time. These results demonstrate how velocity fluctuations, despite leaving the steady state unchanged, can profoundly influence the transient spatial structures and showcase rich non-equilibrium behavior. Our analytical results are corroborated by Monte Carlo simulations, showing an excellent agreement.

</details>

Abstract: Work dissipation into a thermal bath is standard, whereas the reverse process -- sole work extraction from a thermal bath without a net state change -- contradicts the Kelvin-Planck formulation of the second law of thermodynamics. However, this prohibition is bypassed for baths at negative absolute temperature, sparking ongoing debate regarding their thermodynamic stability. Here, we reconcile the second law with sole work extraction from a negative-temperature bath by considering a large virtual assembly of weakly coupled working systems rather than an isolated machine. By assigning this assembly a canonical temperature derived from its Shannon/information entropy, the framework naturally extends to molecular-scale machines. We demonstrate paradigmatic examples where the assembly's canonical temperature is cooler than the negative-temperature bath. Consequently, sole work extraction represents standard heat flow from a hot system (the negative-temperature bath) to a cooler system (the working assembly), rendering the process fully consistent with the second law. This informational and statistical-mechanical framework also naturally explains the counter-intuitive phenomenon of "donkey behavior", where increasing the workload in a negative-temperature bath leads to enhanced work extraction.

</details>

Abstract: Finite-$N$ fluctuations govern the slow relaxation of long-range interacting systems, yet accessing their full statistics demands huge ensembles of costly simulations. We show that, in homogeneous systems without collective effects, this statistical problem collapses onto just three deterministic kinetic equations. Starting from the Landau-Dean-Kawasaki equation, i.e. the stochastic counterpart of the large deviation principle for the Landau equation, we derive the infinite hierarchy of $n$-point moments of the empirical distribution function at leading order in $1/N$. We prove that it is solved exactly by a quasi-Gaussian closure. The resulting minimal Landau fluctuation hierarchy is formed by the Landau equation, a Lyapunov equation for the two-point moment, and a kinetic equation for the three-point moment. From these three equations, all higher-order moments follow algebraically. The hierarchy exactly preserves the collisional invariants and their fluctuations, and predicts the full probability distribution of observables in the central fluctuation regime. Benchmarked against $N$-body realizations, it reproduces the mean, variance and higher-order moments with an accuracy that improves systematically with $N$. A single deterministic time-integration of three coupled partial differential equations thus replaces large ensembles of simulations, for any large $N$.

Abstract: Despite their promise, the targeted synthesis of novel MXenes remains challenging due to their vast structural and compositional space and the lack of reliable stability descriptors that account for synthesis conditions. To address this gap, we utilized density functional theory (DFT) with the r$^2$SCAN functional to develop a high-fidelity dataset of 1,040 ordered binary and ternary bare delaminated MXenes, including mono-transition metal MXenes, in-plane ordered double-transition metal MXenes, in-plane ordered metal-divacancy MXenes, and out-of-plane ordered double-transition-metal MXenes. For each structure, we calculated formation and decomposition Gibbs energies, decomposition products, layer symmetry groups, and energies above the hull. We find that all MXenes are thermodynamically inaccessible under equilibrium conditions. However, 115 predominantly carbide MXenes become thermodynamically stabilized at elevated temperatures (500--2000 K), and 23 are stabilized under high pressure (10 GPa). Additionally, three MXenes in the Mn--Fe--C chemical space are stabilized within an experimentally feasible range of carbon chemical potentials. These findings reveal stability trends across the MXene space and identify synthesis strategies that can guide experimental efforts. Our dataset provides a robust foundation for developing machine-learned stability descriptors and for guiding experimental efforts toward novel MXene discovery. By identifying synthesizable candidates along with their synthesis conditions, this work bridges a critical gap in data-driven approaches to MXene discovery and lays the foundation for future autonomous experimental workflows.

Abstract: Heat dissipation in laser optical materials depends not only on their thermal conductivity but also on how absorbed energy is redistributed among various phonon modes. In calcium fluoride (CaF2), rapid energy transfer from hot electrons to weakly propagating longitudinal optical (LO) phonons can create a nonequilibrium phonon bottleneck effect that limits efficient heat removal. Here, we propose selective phonon excitation as an effective strategy to break this bottleneck. Combining electron-phonon scattering calculations, phonon Boltzmann transport analysis, and nonequilibrium molecular dynamics simulations, we identify LO phonons as the primary modes that preferentially receive energy from hot carriers, leading to energy accumulation due to their inefficient transport and relaxation. Guided by three-phonon scattering phase-space analysis, selective excitation of transverse acoustic (TA) modes can accelerate LO relaxation and promote energy transfer toward heat-carrying acoustic modes. While sustained LO-phonon excitation reduces the thermal conductivity by approximately 12%, additional TA-mode excitation improves heat conduction by approximately 21% compared to the state limited by the LO-phonon bottleneck, surpassing the unexcited value. These findings demonstrate that controlling interbranch energy redistribution can counteract phonon-bottleneck-induced heat accumulation and reveal a working route for active thermal management in laser optical materials through selective control of phonon populations.

</details>

Abstract: Layered magnetic materials with tunable valley and spin degrees of freedom hold promise for multifunctional spintronic devices. Here, we demonstrate that the orientation of an in-plane electric field enables selective switching of single-valley spin splitting and reversal of the anomalous Hall conductivity in strained bilayer MoO. In particular, electric fields along the a and b directions selectively lift the spin degeneracy at the X and Y valleys, respectively, while preserving it at the other valley. These effects are realized in the energetically and dynamically stable AC2 stacking configuration, where biaxial strain induces Dirac-like band crossings that enable subsequent electric-field control. Our findings reveal a combined effect of strain and electric fields for controlling valley-selective spin splitting and anomalous Hall responses, highlighting bilayer MoO as a promising platform for multifunctional spintronic and valleytronic devices.

Within the Born approximation, Fourier transformation over probe position reveals fringes whose frequency encodes the dopant's distance from the focal plane. Two principal challenges arise in extending this approach to realistic experimental conditions. First, dynamical scattering degrades fringe contrast which can be partially mitigated by acquiring off major zone axes. Second, because core-loss doses are punishing, we use a Bayesian framework which, in simulation, achieves nanometre to sub-nanometre depth precision from only 100 to 1000 core-loss counts, depending on the signal-to-background.

Abstract: Surfaces and interfaces govern the performance of wurtzite semiconductors in electronics, sensing, and solar-fuel conversion. To date, first-principles studies have focused mostly on high-symmetry low-index facets, owing to the computational cost and complexity of modelling low-symmetry high-index surfaces. In practice, however, surface formation energy is determined by the planar atomic density and the strength of the bonds severed upon cleavage, neither of which inherently excludes high-Miller-index surfaces from being energetically competitive. Here, we combine automated surface generation, machine-learning interatomic potential screening, and density functional theory to explore 226 clean, stoichiometric surface models of wurtzite GaN, LaN, and ZnO, spanning all Miller orientations with $|h|,|k|,|l| \le 3$ and their inequivalent terminations. We find that low-symmetry high-index prismatic facets are energetically competitive with respect to the conventional $m$- and $a$-planes: nine of the fifteen lowest-energy nonpolar surfaces are high-index, and the five best candidates of each compound lie within only 2.5-5.3% of the minimum surface energy. Hybrid-functional calculations reveal, however, that this energetic proximity does not translate into similar electronic alignment. In GaN, the two most stable surfaces differ by just 0.08 J m$^{-2}$ in formation energy but by almost 0.8 eV in vacuum-referenced band-edge positions, whereas in LaN the nearly degenerate $m$- and $a$-planes are separated by 0.16 eV. Wurtzite facets with similar formation energies may therefore exhibit very different ionization potentials and electron affinities. Our results enable the joint, systematic assessment of stability and band alignment across the facets that can actually occur in wurtzites, and provide a map of candidate surfaces for the rational design of related nanostructure and heterointerfaces.

</details>

Asked for an air-stable alternative, it designs one and tests it against new structures from the generative model. With web search alone, the same agent proposes what the literature already discusses. Put together, the data, the models and the reaction graph let a general agent do what none of them does alone: go from a question in plain words to an experiment that a laboratory can run and test.

Abstract: The influence of defect topology and chemical doping on the optical response of amorphous silicon (a-Si) remains unresolved. In this study, hybrid-functional density functional theory and linear-response calculations are employed to examine four structurally distinct continuous random networks with substitutional boron and phosphorus doping. Tauc and optical sum-rule analyses further indicate that network topology defines the broader optical landscape, whereas doping primarily affects states near the gap and redistributes spectral weight. These findings establish defect topology as a microscopic control parameter that links amorphous structure, dopant activity, and optical response in amorphous silicon.

Abstract: Universal density functional theory (DFT) Hamiltonian models trained across broad chemical and structural spaces offer an efficient route from atomic structure to electronic properties. However, estimating prediction uncertainty alongside fast Hamiltonian predictions remains a challenge. In this work, we present EviDTB, an evidential deep regression approach for tight-binding DFT Hamiltonians that effectively estimates prediction uncertainty in a single forward pass. Compared with deep ensembles, Monte Carlo dropout, and mean-variance estimation (MVE), EviDTB achieves a better balance between computational cost and uncertainty-quantification performance. On 5,478 held-out test structures, EviDTB achieves a full spin--orbit-coupled (SOC) Hamiltonian matrix-element mean absolute error of 0.622meV. Its mean uncertainty per atom correlates with the mean local squared prediction error (Spearman $ρ=0.812$); removing the 15% most uncertain structures reduces the 99th percentile of this error by 78%, from 16.63 to 3.72eV$^2$. This score offers a practical way to prioritize candidates for first-principles verification. We hope that combining fast Hamiltonian prediction with uncertainty-guided screening will accelerate the search for materials with desirable electronic properties.

Abstract: Knowledge of phase stability is the first requirement for materials design, and predicting the conditions under which new materials can be synthesized has become a key capability of self-driving laboratories (SDLs). For inorganic materials, notably alloys, these predictions come from thermodynamic databases built with the CALPHAD method. Building a database, however, requires expert judgment at many steps, and because these decisions are rarely recorded, others can use a database but cannot regenerate or update it. Machines can already collect the data, build a first model with machine-learned interatomic potentials and refine it against experiments. The assessment itself, the sequence of judgments that turns these parts into a reliable database, is still carried out by human experts. We argue that it can be automated reproducibly only if the expert knowledge behind an assessment is encoded in formal protocols, so that an assessment delivers a record of its construction and not only its parameters. We introduce ORCHID-SAGE, a standard that organizes this record into reproducible and automatable protocols. In this first attempt, applied to Cr-Ni, the database was regenerated from its record by a script on a second machine, by an AI agent working only through the Model Context Protocol (MCP) and by a refit from public sources only, with the same predictions. Recorded in this way, a database need no longer be fixed at publication but can be updated by AI agents as SDLs and other sources provide new evidence, while the judgment remains with people and on the record.

</details>

Abstract: Tiling mathematics has twice become the language of a state of matter: crystals, then quasicrystals, whose thermodynamics are described by the random tiling of fixed shapes. Metric disorder thus enters the exact-counting paradigm, and is identifiable in real materials by Bragg spots wrapped in metric halos.

Depending on the assumptions made, this could correspond to magnetizations as high as $μ_0 M = 0.3 T$ or as low as 4 $μT$. However, when measuring the magnetic stray field outside the driven sample we unambiguously determine that the magnetization in SiC is negligibly small ($μ_0 M \lt 0.001 T$).

Abstract: Krylov complexity has become a central probe of operator growth, scrambling, and quantum chaos. Yet, a basic fact hinders its experimental study: laboratory quantum systems are routinely driven in time, whereas the Krylov construction assumes a fixed generator and breaks down once the Hamiltonian varies. Here, we solve the time-dependent many-body Krylov problem exactly and in closed form for scale- and rotation-invariant systems realized in current quantum-simulation and control platforms. We find exact relations that map the driven dynamics onto mutually orthogonal Krylov subspaces and yield a closed-form spread complexity, determined solely by the spectral properties of the initial Hamiltonian and the driving schedule. The construction extends to Liouville space and solves the time-dependent density-matrix Krylov problem, which makes the Krylov dynamics of initial thermal states experimentally accessible. In scale-invariant quantum gases, such as the driven Lieb-Liniger model, the Tonks-Girardeau gas, and the Calogero-Sutherland model, the complexity is set by the width of the atomic cloud and its rate of change, directly accessible via in situ and time-of-flight imaging. In rotation-invariant spin systems such as the Haldane-Shastry and central-spin models, it is governed by collective spin precession that can be probed and read out in trapped-ion, cavity-QED, and circuit-QED platforms.

Abstract: We investigate quantum gate teleportation as a primitive for satellite-enabled distributed quantum computing. In the considered architecture, a satellite distributes entangled photon pairs to two optical ground stations, which transfer the shared quantum resource to remote quantum processing units. This entanglement is then used to implement a nonlocal controlled-NOT gate between two circuit qubits located in different processors. We formulate the minimal four-qubit protocol, consisting of two network qubits that provide the entangled resource and two circuit qubits on which the distributed gate is performed. Atmospheric propagation effects in the gravitational field are modeled through an effective two-qubit depolarizing channel whose parameters depend on atmospheric and optical quantities, including the Fried parameter and receiver aperture. The performance of the protocol is quantified by the fidelity between the ideal CNOT output state and the state produced by noisy quantum gate teleportation. Our results show that the fidelity is governed by the quality of the distributed entanglement and depends jointly on atmospheric coherence, aperture size, and effective depolarization strength. These findings connect physical-layer modeling of satellite optical channels with gate-level performance in distributed quantum computing.

Abstract: How much memory does a quantum device need to prepare systems one at a time? We address this question through the lens of quantum resource theories, which formalize physical restrictions on how the memory may interact with the stream of systems it processes. By comparing sequential with collective preparation, we show that under general assumptions both have the same asymptotic memory cost per output. Still, sequential preparation can require an unboundedly larger memory than collective one, and we investigate how this gap depends on the physical restrictions on the allowed interactions. Abstract: Quantum reinforcement learning (QRL) has emerged as a promising approach for controlling quantum systems and accelerating learning tasks with quantum resources. However, most existing QRL formulations still retain a classical reinforcement learning interface: states and actions are often encoded in computational bases and rewards are extracted through measurements. This interface is restrictive for genuinely quantum environments: choosing a measurement can discard entanglement information, while repeated intermediate measurements collapse the evolving state and require costly re-preparation. This motivates a fully QRL framework in which the agent, environment, rewards, and return accumulation are all quantum. In this paper, we take a step toward this goal and introduce a new QRL framework in which both the policy and the environment are modeled by conditional quantum channels. We then propose a quantum policy-gradient algorithm for parameterized conditional quantum channels with a trainable control basis. We also derive finite- and infinite-horizon gradient estimators using a new parameter-shift rule. This rule is needed because policy parameters are shared across repeated interaction blocks and the infinite-horizon objective is not a finite circuit; rather than shifting every occurrence and evaluating the whole trajectory, our estimator shifts only one sampled policy block. Sampling this block from a geometric distribution yields an unbiased gradient estimator under bounded rewards. Finally, we establish convergence of the resulting stochastic gradient-ascent procedure to stationary points under standard smoothness and step-size assumptions.

Abstract: Where practical advantage emerges in quantum dynamics simulation remains a central debate in quantum computing. Claimed advantages can be rapidly narrowed or overturned by stronger classical methods, while progress on both sides can outpace the journal publication cycle. We present QSim-Arena, a research framework in which AI agents combine a closed loop of verification and benchmarking with open exploration. Hamiltonians, initial states and accuracy targets for evolved states or observable expectation values provide explicit specifications for evaluating simulation methods. Agents translate published techniques into executable components, reproduce reported results and benchmark their performance across diverse Hamiltonian families. Competing classical methods are implemented and independently crosschecked, providing credible baselines for end-to-end quantum resource estimates based on rigorous error bounds and empirical calibration. Alongside this closed loop, agents recombine classical simulation techniques and co-design fault-tolerant quantum circuits using knowledge extracted from the literature. Initial applications improve baseline resource estimates through the integration of established techniques. QSim-Arena thus treats quantum advantage assessment as an evolving, reproducible research process that can respond to advances in algorithms, error analysis and hardware.

</details>

Abstract: How well a superposition of nonorthogonal states, such as an optical cat state, serves as a resource depends on how its components interfere. Here we determine the optimal probability of converting one such superposition into another by superposition-free operations. These operations cannot make the components of a superposition interfere more destructively with certainty while keeping all of them, and this principle fixes the optimal probability for every source that is symmetric under permutations of its components. When the reference states are coherent states, as in a cat state, every operation that maps each of them to a coherent state, such as photon loss or photon subtraction, is superposition-free. For two reference states we solve the problem completely. Superpositions with coefficients of equal modulus, the counterparts of the maximally coherent states of coherence theory, are therefore inequivalent resources: an odd cat state becomes even with certainty by a normalized photon subtraction, whereas an even one becomes odd only with a probability that vanishes for small amplitudes, and the even and the Yurke$-$Stoler cat states cannot be converted into each other with certainty in either direction. Collective operations on several copies can do better than converting them one at a time, except in the distillation of coherence, which gains nothing from them. For three or more reference states with equal positive overlaps, no state reaches every other state with certainty; the equal-modulus superpositions that interfere most destructively still yield a maximally coherent state with certainty and reach every superposition of all reference states at least as well as such a state does, but in the worst case no better.

</details>

Abstract: The question of local encodability asks: can a given, multipartite pure quantum state be completed to an orthonormal basis using only local unitary rotations? If any such state in a given system has this property, it is called universally locally encodable (ULE), a feature, which has applications in quantum information theory, engineering and math. Despite its easy formulation, the answer to this question depends nontrivially on the local dimensions of the system: While universal local encodability is known for $2\times 2$, $4\times 4$ and $8\times 8$-dimensional systems, it was so-far only conjectured to hold in $3\times 3$, and non-ULE was recently proved for systems of four and more qubits by Pauwels and Gühne.
  We resolve the open conjecture by proving universality for two qutrits. Furthermore, we construct (among others) universal local encodings in dimensions $d\times2$ for $d\ge2$, in dimensions $d\times3$ for $d=3,4$ and $d\ge6$, and in every bipartite system with one or two infinite-dimensional subsystems. For finite-dimensional multipartite systems, we turn a heuristic parameter count into a necessary condition for universality, which excludes universal local encodability in infinitely many cases. Finally, we give universal local encodability an operational interpretation by showing that non-ULE is equivalent to whether general measurements yield an advantage over projectively simulable ones in discrimination tasks where unknown, random local unitary rotations have to be detected.

</details>

Abstract: The Mpemba effect, in which an initially hotter system relaxes faster than a colder one, has been widely studied in both classical and quantum systems, yet identifying the specific effects of quantization requires a consistent comparison with the corresponding classical dynamics. Our findings reveal that quantization fundamentally unlocks anomalous relaxation pathways, rather than merely modifying relaxation rates.

Abstract: Multiparameter pure-state metrology is limited by the incompatibility of measurements optimized for different parameters. We characterize uniform sensitivity by the Fisher floor, the smallest fraction of the quantum Fisher information (QFI) retained over all pure states and parameter directions. We prove that a constant number of fixed orthonormal bases suffices for a nearly optimal Fisher floor, independently of the Hilbert-space dimension $d$. With high probability, five independent Haar bases already achieve a positive, dimension-independent floor, and larger constant families achieve approximate universal Fisher symmetry: they retain nearly one half of the QFI in every direction, the largest uniform fraction permitted by measurement incompatibility. The required number of bases is optimal up to a logarithmic factor in inverse accuracy. In contrast, $d+1$ mutually unbiased bases, when they exist, can minimize mean Fisher anisotropy among families of $d+1$ bases yet have a Fisher floor of at most $1/(d+1)$, showing that optimal average balance does not ensure uniform sensitivity.

Abstract: Electronic structure calculations become computationally expensive on classical computers as electron correlation increases. The choice of ansatz plays a pivotal role in the performance and scalability of the VQE, yet selecting an optimal ansatz remains challenging. Existing approaches including chemically inspired ansatz, adaptive schemes and Hardware Efficient Ansätze (HEA) lack a unified comparison. This gap makes the scalability and trade-offs of each method unclear for varying molecular sizes.
  This work presents a systematic benchmarking study comparing Unitary Couples Cluster Singles and Doubles Ansatz (UCCSD), fermionic ADAPT-VQE, BasicPCA HEA, and Excitation Preserving HEA across molecules of varying size like $H_2$, $LiH$, $N_2$. We evaluate trade-offs among circuit depth, parameter count, ground-state energy error, and NISQ feasibility. ADAPT-VQE achieves accuracy comparable to UCCSD with lower resource overhead, but becomes impractical for modest system sizes ($\sim$10 electrons) due to increasing circuit complexity. In contrast, HEA reduces hardware costs but requires more parameters and sacrifices accuracy, with performance strongly dependent on molecular size. The Excitation Preserving ansatz remains below chemical accuracy for $H_2$ and $LiH$, and fails for $N_2$, underscoring the need to balance expressibility and trainability. Overall, these results highlight key trade-offs between accuracy and resource efficiency across VQE ansätze, emphasizing the need to improve ADAPT-VQE scalability and HEA accuracy while addressing optimization challenges for near-term quantum hardware.

Abstract: Noise degrades quantum coherence and limits the precision of quantum sensors. Quantum control can counteract these effects, but existing strategies typically assume that the noise is known. In practice, noise is often only partially characterized, so its parameters must be estimated jointly with the Hamiltonian parameters of interest. Here we show that uncertainty in the noise parameters can destroy otherwise attainable Heisenberg scaling, even when coherence is preserved. For general Markovian dynamics under arbitrary quantum control, we derive a necessary and sufficient condition for each Hamiltonian parameter to retain this scaling and determine its optimal asymptotic precision when the condition holds. When the condition fails, we propose a protocol that uses a suitable noisy reference system to restore this scaling, and prove that a reference insensitive to the Hamiltonian parameters but subject to an independent copy of the sensor's noise recovers the optimal precision attainable with known noise. Our results reveal how incomplete knowledge of the noise affects the fundamental limits of quantum metrology, with broad implications for achieving quantum-enhanced precision in realistic environments.

Abstract: Discovering new materials with targeted properties is a central goal of materials science, yet the space of candidates is vast. A material's properties follow from its Hamiltonian, and a quantum computer can calculate them from it. Abstract: In nanolasers, a few to a few hundred quantum emitters share a single mode of light. Coupled identically to the mode, the emitters do not radiate independently but form collective bright and dark states, producing superradiant bursts and strongly bunched photon statistics below the laser threshold. We investigate to what extent this behavior can be understood as random jumps between collective Dicke states. From the laser master equation, we derive a hierarchy of models defined by the polarization order, i.e., the number of photons involved in the coherence between the emitters and the light, and include the coherence to various orders. With all of the coherences eliminated, we obtain a Dicke Markov chain, a stochastic jump process over the photon number and the Dicke states, in which collective effects enter only through enhanced or suppressed transition rates. It predicts superthermal bunching and a strong even--odd dependence on the emitter number. It recovers about 80\% of the bunching in the most coherent regime, where conventional rate equations predict no superthermal bunching. Keeping the first polarization order, i.e., the optical polarization, and eliminating the second order, i.e., the two-photon coherences, yields a population-polarization model, which recovers the bunching of the full master equation almost perfectly. This hierarchy of polarization orders is an alternative to the cumulant expansion and, unlike it, recovers the master equation at the highest order. As a numerically exact reference, a symmetry-reduced master equation extends the simulation range all the way to 34 (24) emitters below (above) threshold. The Dicke Markov chain can be solved directly for hundreds of emitters and sampled for thousands of emitters, further extending the range of numerical methods. We compare the numerical performance of the methods in detail to guide the choice of technique.

</details>

Abstract: The quantum switch applies two operations in a coherent superposition of their causal orders and is a simple example of indefinite causal order. No quantum circuit that uses each operation once in a definite order can even describe it, yet photonic experiments have demonstrated its physical realization. We ask how many calls to the black-box operations a quantum computer in the circuit model needs to reproduce the switch. If one of the black boxes is known to be unitary, three calls suffice. For general channels on a $d$-dimensional system, we prove three results. In all three, circuits may use classical feed-forward and unlimited ancillary quantum memory. First, no finite number of calls suffices for exact simulation in the worst case. Second, approximation to any fixed error below $1/2$, measured in half the diamond norm, requires $Ω(d^{1/4})$ calls, exponential in the number of qubits. Third, an ordinary circuit can require exponentially many calls to obtain a single bit that the switch extracts with a few uses. We consider a device whose actual noise is dephasing in a fixed unknown basis and test it against a theoretical depolarizing model. The two channels have the same entanglement and average gate fidelities, so these figures alone do not distinguish them. At fixed noise strength, the quantum switch tells them apart with a number of device uses independent of $d$, whereas the circuit model requires $Ω(d^{1/16})$ calls. Noise characterization is needed for quantum error correction, and this gives a provable query advantage of the switch in a task relevant to practical quantum computing. We invite future work that extends such advantages to more practical computing tasks.

Abstract: When a quantum processor is reached through a cloud queue, a variational algorithm pays for every round trip, and a single round trip can carry many circuits. The classical optimizer therefore decides how much is learned per job, not only per shot. We study this for a quantum neural network classifier. We compare optimizers that are given the same number of jobs and the same shots per job, and that differ only in how they fill each job. This comparison treats hybrid quantum-classical training as an execution-efficiency problem: how to obtain useful optimization progress from each costly interaction with a quantum processor. The resulting perspective connects optimizer design to overhead amortization and efficient use of accelerator resources. A direct port of a batched pattern search that worked for the quantum approximate optimization algorithm stalls once the model has a few dozen parameters. What does transfer is the principle behind it: fill each job with a batch of parameter vectors that is fixed before the job is sent. For circuits built from Pauli rotations the natural batch is the parameter-shift stencil. We show that its gradient error falls as the inverse of the shots per job, while the error of simultaneous-perturbation methods (SPSA) has a floor that no number of shots removes. In simulation, batched parameter-shift training needs between a fifth and a half of the jobs SPSA needs, for 24 to 48 parameters, and the ordering holds at two noise levels. A new structure-aware stencil search reads the same kind of rows as sinusoids and also beats SPSA. On IBM's ibm_fez processor, in three interleaved runs at six and eight qubits, the batched optimizer reached SPSA's final training loss within 4 to 20 of its 50 jobs. Its test accuracy was clearly higher at eight qubits; at six qubits the difference was not significant. All hardware numbers can be re-derived from the raw shots.

Abstract: In prior work we showed that a reinforcement-learning eavesdropper can intercept BB84 quantum key distribution while holding the quantum bit error rate below the abort threshold and keeping a usable distillable key. We cast the interaction as an alternating best-response game between a tabular eavesdropper and a tabular threshold-adapting detector. The exploitability does not approach a fixed point, and its round-to-round swings do not decay. Two controls, one retraining the attacker against a frozen defender and one retraining the defender against a frozen attacker, show that the swings on each side match the variance of the best-response procedure itself. The dynamics fluctuate at the scale of best-response variance rather than converging, with neither player settling to a fixed response. The behavior is noise-dependent, and the stealth window stays open at the low noise where practical QKD operates. The defender contains the attacker on average at intermediate noise without the contest settling, and at high noise the channel rather than the learned policy suppresses the attack. As a candidate mechanism we examine a last-mover information asymmetry, supported by significant anti-persistence at intermediate noise and consistent with best-response noise at low noise. The results suggest that a learned detector cannot be certified from any single round, and that closing the window calls for breaking the information structure rather than out-adapting the attacker.

Abstract: Grover's algorithm searches an unstructured database with a number of oracle queries that grows only as the square root of its size. Noise erodes this advantage, and we ask, analytically, how quickly. When a depolarizing channel acts on the whole register after every gate layer, it commutes with the gates, and the noisy state is the noiseless one with a coherent weight that decays geometrically with depth. The Grover rotation is not slowed. Its weight decays, the best stopping point moves earlier, and the expected query cost keeps its square-root scaling only while the accumulated noise over the circuit stays small. For independent errors on the qubits we show that the ideal Grover operator freezes any amplitude pushed out of the two-dimensional search plane. This gives the exact effect of a single error: phase flips do most damage early in the search, bit flips late, and combined errors at any time. For large databases it also reduces the noisy search to Bloch-type equations for the part of the state that stays in the plane, in which the algorithm is a rotation and the noise appears as leakage and dephasing. We derive the rates for bit-flip, phase-flip, depolarizing, and amplitude-damping noise and solve the equations. For coherent errors in the rotation angle we bound the success probability by a band that widens linearly with depth. Abstract: The Leggett-Garg (LG) inequalities were formulated for testing the incompatibility between the classical worldview of macrorealism and quantum theory. By analogy with the Tsirelson bound for Bell inequalities, the maximal quantum violation of LG inequalities is commonly referred to as the temporal Tsirelson bound (TTB). Nevertheless, several studies have claimed that, unlike the Tsirelson bound, the TTB can be exceeded within quantum theory. In particular, by employing degeneracy-breaking measurement in [\href{ https://doi.org/10.1103/PhysRevLett.113.050401}{Phys. Rev. Lett. \textbf{113}, 050401 (2014)}] and by using the superposition of unitaries in [\href{https://doi.org/10.1103/vydp-9qqq}{Phys. Rev. In this work, we critically examine the reasoning underlying these purported TTB violations and show that the corresponding arguments suffer from conceptual shortcomings. We further show that once these conceptual issues are addressed appropriately, the macrorealist bound of LG inequality reaches the algebraic maximum, and hence no violation of the TTB is obtained. In particular, we demonstrate that the proposed TTB violations rely on frameworks that go beyond the standard LG framework, and therefore are of no relevance for testing the incompatibility between macrorealism and quantum theory.

Abstract: Krylov spread complexity quantifies how extensively a quantum system explores its Hilbert space during time evolution. We identify two classes of unitary transformations that preserve the complexity of the associated dynamics, and relate this property to the invariance of the energy distribution of the initial state and of its associated Hamiltonian moments. Abstract: Computation of eigenvalues and eigenvectors of a sparse Hermitian matrix is a fundamental problem in algebra, arising both as a problem of independent interest and as a key subroutine in a wide range of computational and scientific applications. In this work, we develop a quantum algorithm that determines the spectrum of a sparse Hermitian matrix, defined as the set of its distinct eigenvalues, and for each eigenvalue, prepares a corresponding eigenvector as a quantum state in its eigenspace. The eigenvalues are represented as binary strings with a prescribed precision, while the corresponding eigenvectors are kept as quantum states, eliminating the need to reconstruct their classical counterparts. The algorithm instead enables estimation of expectation values $\braket{x|M|x}$, where $\ket{x}$ is a prepared eigenstate and $M$ is an observable. For an $n \times n$ $s$-sparse Hermitian matrix $H$, dense methods based on Householder tridiagonalization and QR iteration compute all eigenvalues and eigenvectors in $\mathcal{O}(n^3)$ time. Exploiting sparsity, the Lanczos method computes all eigenvalues in $\mathcal{O}(n^2 s)$ time in exact arithmetic, but recovering all eigenvectors still requires $\mathcal{O}(n^3)$ time. In contrast, our algorithm runs in time $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(n s^2 \log^2 n / Δ_{\min})$, where $Δ_{\min}$ denotes the minimum spectral gap of the normalized matrix. For inverse gaps up to $Δ_{\min}^{-1} = \mathcal{O}(n)$, the most favorable scaling for $n$ distinct eigenvalues, attained by nearly uniformly spaced spectra such as those of perfect-state-transfer spin chains and the Sylvester-Kac matrix, and for sparsity up to $s = o(\sqrt{n}/\log n)$, the proposed algorithm achieves an improved asymptotic runtime over the classical $\mathcal{O}(n^3)$ approach for computing all eigenvalues and eigenvectors, under standard sparse-access assumptions.

Abstract: Ground-state spin squeezing is a resource for quantum-enhanced measurements, but is commonly associated with a closing excitation gap near a quantum critical point. A closing gap can complicate state preparation and reduce thermal robustness. Here we identify the physical mechanism linking squeezing to collective excitations and establish when stronger squeezing accompanies a larger many-body gap. Using the anisotropic Lipkin--Meshkov--Glick model, we show that, for a quadratic collective mode, the ratio of two transverse fluctuation costs determines squeezing, whereas their geometric mean sets the collective gap. The pair terms responsible for unequal costs are allowed, but not guaranteed, by $\mathbb Z_2$ parity. To extend this picture beyond a single mode, we derive an exact relation between squeezing, polarization, and excitation energies weighted by their collective-spin transition strengths. For XXZ chains, weak-coupling and spin-wave analyses explain how squeezing can develop while both the collective and many-body gaps increase. Exact finite-size calculations for power-law interactions, including the nearest-neighbor limit, confirm a negative-coupling branch on which the two gaps coincide and increase as squeezing improves. For nearest-neighbor interactions, this branch also retains squeezing at higher temperatures than a gap-closing branch with comparable ground-state squeezing. In the Ising chain, by contrast, a staggered excitation can lower the many-body gap without directly contributing to collective fluctuations. Our results explain why ground-state squeezing can accompany either a closing or an opening collective gap, and identify a gap-opening branch with enhanced thermal robustness and the potential for faster adiabatic preparation.

Abstract: Continuous quantum error correction promises reduced thresholds and hardware overhead compared to the digital paradigm, but its complex model estimation has greatly reduced its practicality. The reduced model exposes and corrects two failure mechanisms of the feedback loop. Strong monitoring damps the rotational partner through which the controller steers the stabilizer and injects back-action diffusion along the recovery path; lowering the measurement strength during correction raises the entanglement fidelity of partner-steering LQR control by $0.096$ in the three-qubit code with three monitored parities. Linearizing at the codespace leaves the syndrome uncontrollable, so the controller can stall; a gain designed at a shifted operating point removes this degeneracy. In the limit of zero measurement during correction, we prove that a syndrome-only filter is exact at every recovery boundary. The resulting committed-recovery controller needs only $2^{\,n-k}$ class probabilities and gives the highest entanglement fidelity compared among the methods tested in the literature, $0.947$ against $0.920$ for a measurement-based estimator protocol in the three-qubit bit-flip code, and $0.726$ vs at most $0.407$ for continuous controllers in the five-qubit perfect code.
