Abstract: Non-convex optimization plays an important role in modern machine learning since plenty of real-world problems have objective functions or constraints with non-convex properties. The non-convex nature of landscapes gives rise to numerous local minima and saddle points, dramatically hindering the ability of gradient descent-based methods to explore the landscape and obtain global optima. Here we present quantum tunneling search (QTS), an algorithm based on quantum tunneling walk and local annealing, which effectively explores the non-convex landscape by leveraging the quantum tunneling effect. In experiments, we map an objective function to the potential term of the waveguide Hamiltonian by controlling the refractive index with the femtosecond direct laser writing technique. With a proper input state, the average probability distributions on 10-micrometer-spaced 29 x 1 and 21 x 9 waveguide arrays highlight the local minima of various 1D and 2D non-convex landscapes, respectively. By further introducing the varying Hamiltonian approach, more efficient search and even the identification of the global minimum are achieved. Our work experimentally verifies that QTS is a successful and efficient non-convex optimization solver, demonstrating quantum speedups over classical stochastic gradient descent (SGD), which serves as a practical application of near-term analog quantum simulators in the field of machine learning.

Abstract: The development of quantum computing has opened up opportunities to re-explore conventional game models by the integration of quantum processes. Through the established framework of quantum game theory, we examine the problem of speculative pricing in the Keynesian Beauty Contest model -- the $p$-Beauty Contest ($p$-BC). In the $p$-BC, players are rewarded with the optimal payoff for successfully bidding at some fraction $p$ of the average of all of the players' bids. Consequently, \textit{rational} plays in the $p$-BC inexorably lead to boom or bust strategies for $p>1$ or $p<1$ respectively. We demonstrate a quantum continuous-variable implementation of $p$-BC where the boom-and-bust rational behavior is mitigated through a global transformation of the players' bids generated by increasing multimode squeezing. While we focus on a quantum mechanical implementation of the $p$-BC, we show that an entirely analogous game can be formulated via classical channels by a global mediator. Thus we identify global mediation as the underlying game-theoretic mechanism for quenching boom and bust phenomena in our model. We analyze the quantum $p$-BC for two, three, and many players in a mean-field type implementation. We derive an analytical form for the probability distribution of the payoff under single-value measurements of the quantum processing circuit, and explore how the rational behavior differs from the expected-value measurement setting.

Abstract: The prevailing wisdom asserts that in the Holevo Frenkel Weiner (HFW) framework of communication tasks, a qubit offers no advantage over a c bit unless additional quantum resources are supplied or the classical shared randomness (SR) is restricted. In contrast, we establish the supremacy of qubit communication in the HFW framework when additional quantum resources, such as entanglement or measurement incompatibility are not injected and SR is a free resource. We consider a prepare-measure scenario where the sender, Alice, prepares six states subject to a specific cryptographic constraint, and the receiver, Bob, performs a three-outcome measurement. Such a constraint on preparation impacts the power of qubit communication quite differently than that of c-bit communication, ultimately giving rise to the quantum advantage. We introduce a witness capable of distinguishing quantum strategies that lie beyond the set of possible classical strategies and demonstrate the optimal quantum advantage. {As an application, we demonstrate the generation of certified randomness without requiring any seed randomness, since Bob has no input.} We further analyse the robustness of the witness and show that the advantage persists in noisy situations, making it suitable for experimental realisation. Finally, we demonstrate that Bob's three outcome qubit positive operator valued-measure (POVMs) has a distinct capability of detecting coherence, which can be regarded as the non-classical feature responsible for showcasing the quantum advantage.

</details>

Abstract: Drive-modified dissipation in multilevel quantum systems is not, in general, accurately captured by conventional master equations. We derive a unified master equation using Lewis-Riesenfeld dynamical invariants, accommodating arbitrary driving within Born-Markov theory. Abstract: In solids, the quantum geometric tensor (QGT), $Q_{μν}=g_{μν}-\frac{i}{2} Ω_{μν}$, combines the quantum metric and Berry curvature. Its imaginary part governs anomalous velocity and quantized Hall transport, while its real part determines the quantum weight and geometric contributions to flat-band superfluid stiffness and dielectric response. Here, we extend the QGT to four-dimensional (4D) energy-momentum space ($ω/c,k$), treating frequency and momentum as independent coordinates. Invariance of the real-spacetime plane-wave phase fixes their Lorentz transformation in energy-momentum space. Lorentz boosts of static Dirac and Weyl systems then generate frequency metrics, quadrupolar frequency-momentum shear metrics, and electric-like Berry-Maxwell fields. We identify five Lorentz-invariant contractions and derive the governing field equations and flow conservation laws for both the quantum metric and Berry curvature from static Weyl geometry and Lorentz covariance. We further connect Gaussian spectral amplitudes of the QGT to localized Gaussian events in 4D spacetime. Finally, we discuss experimental realizations in spacetime crystals or time-varying media and clarify how this unified quantum geometry of a gravitational-like metric and electromagnetic-like gauge fields connects geometric self-consistency with physical event dynamics.

Abstract: In quantum parameter estimation, time-dependent Hamiltonians can enable ultraprecise estimates of a physical parameter beyond the standard Heisenberg limit, also called the super-Heisenberg limit. However, achieving optimal scaling of the measurement precision typically requires an adaptive quantum control scheme which is resource-intensive and hard to implement in general. Furthermore, this advantage is often severely eroded by environmental noise. We then establish conditions and provide performance guarantees for when time dependent Hamiltonians undergoing subspace twirling can achieve super-Heisenberg scaling without adaptive controls. Finally, we illustrate our results using an explicit example where a qubit sensor undergoing a subspace twirl simultaneously suppresses noise and achieves super-Heisenberg scaling. These results offer guidance for the design of ultraprecise quantum sensors under time-dependent and noisy conditions.

Abstract: Can the spatial correlations of a quantum environment be revealed through work extraction? Our results establish Gaussian ergotropy as an operational probe of spatial correlations in bosonic environments.

Abstract: Absolutely maximally entangled (AME) states are pure multipartite states with maximally mixed reductions on every set of at most half the parties. We construct explicit families in local dimension $2q$, twice an odd prime power $q$, using an established coupling of qubits and $q$-level systems. The $q$-level states remove interference between branches, so the qubit states need maximally mixed reductions only on average. This gives AME$(9,2q)$ and AME$(11,2q)$ for every odd prime power $q\geq5$, AME$(13,2q)$ for $q\geq17$, AME$(15,2q)$ for $q\geq9$, and AME$(17,2q)$ for $q\geq67$. For every odd $N\geq3$, the same coupling gives AME$(N,2q)$ under the sufficient condition $q\geq\max\{N,2^{N-2}\}$ on the odd prime power $q$. Within this coupling, the smallest such $q$ must grow exponentially with $N$. Combined with known inputs and products, these families complete the nine-party classification: for integer $d\geq2$, an AME$(9,d)$ state exists if and only if $d\geq3$. Choosing the branch pairing also gives AME$(15,2p)$ states with a cyclic shift of the parties compensated by local unitaries, for every prime $p\equiv1\pmod{15}$.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated the ability to generate parameters for variational quantum circuits without conventional optimization. However, the conditions under which this approach is effective remain unclear. Here, we investigate a frozen, general-purpose LLM that generates complete circuit parameter vectors from symbolic circuit and Hamiltonian descriptions, with optional iterative energy feedback. We evaluate the approach on general random Hamiltonians, structured parent Hamiltonians, and molecular systems. For general Hamiltonians, LLM-generated parameters exhibit larger energy errors than conventional Adam optimization, while additional feedback generally provides limited improvement. In contrast, for structured Hamiltonians, the LLM can generate promising low-energy parameter configurations, suggesting that identifiable physical and mathematical structure facilitates parameter inference. We also show that direct LLM predictions of minimum energies can violate the Hamiltonian spectral lower bound, whereas generated circuit parameters allow independent evaluation of physically realizable energy expectation values. Finally, we investigate blind circuit interpretation and find that the LLM can predict measurement outcomes even when it fails to identify the underlying quantum algorithm. These findings highlight the role of problem structure, the limitations of iterative LLM optimization, and the importance of independently verifiable predictions in quantum parameter programming.

Conventional Gaussian-modulated CV-QKD encodes information in both optical-field quadratures, increasing requirements for state preparation, modulation, and calibration, while potentially introducing additional preparation imperfections. Unidimensional (1D) Gaussian modulation offers a simpler alternative by encoding information in only one quadrature, while the conjugate quadrature constrains the protocol's security. Here, we experimentally demonstrate a 1D Gaussian-modulated CV-QKD system over 12~km of standard single-mode fiber using a local local oscillator (LLO) architecture with independent lasers and an integrated offline classical post-processing chain. The 1D system achieves a privacy-amplified rate of $1.421$~Mbit/s under the asymptotic security treatment and $0.364$~Mbit/s under the finite-size scenario. These results establish an experimental baseline for the performance--complexity trade-off associated with unidimensional Gaussian modulation and provide an experimental basis for developing lower-complexity CV-QKD transmitters.

Abstract: Phenomenological models of quantum-gravity-induced decoherence can leave observable effects even in experiments that do not rely on interferometric measurements. We study three such models, based respectively on deformed relativistic symmetries, stochastic metric fluctuations, and a stochastic generalized uncertainty principle, all of which induce decoherence in the momentum basis. For a free particle, the models preserve momentum distribution and mean energy but produce position diffusion and momentum-space decoherence with model-specific momentum dependences. For a harmonic oscillator, the noncommutativity of the collapse operators with the oscillator Hamiltonian results in anomalous heating, with the three models leading to qualitatively different energy dynamics. The first gives heating at a constant rate, the second at a rate proportional to the kinetic energy, which can lead to exponential growth, and the third at a rate growing cubically with excitation. We further derive the stationary position power spectral density and show that all three models produce the same leading-order excess lineshape in the weak-noise regime, distinct from that of force noise. Comparing with existing mechanical platforms, we find that displacement-noise measurements can already constrain the fluctuation time of the first model below the Planck time, while occupation measurements could improve the bounds on the second model by up to fourteen orders of magnitude over those from ground-state heating of levitated particles. Summing the heating over all phonon modes of a solid, the residual heat release of low-temperature experiments constrains the first model far below the Planck time and the second at the Planck time. Our results establish heating and continuous measurements as complementary non-interferometric probes of gravity-related decoherence.

Abstract: Pauli propagation simulates quantum circuits and many-body dynamics by evolving observables in the Heisenberg picture as sparse sums of Pauli strings. As Pauli-string operations reduce to bit manipulations, its performance is limited by memory bandwidth and capacity rather than computational throughput. Pooling the memory of many cores and nodes is hindered by the deduplication step after every gate, which can require global synchronization. We avoid this bottleneck by partitioning the strings with an $\mathbb{F}_2$-linear hash function, which guarantees that a $k$-qubit channel couples each partition to at most $4^k$ partitions fixed by the gate, decomposing gate application into independent tasks with bounded communication. Benchmarks of our open-source implementation demonstrate efficient parallel scaling within and across compute nodes, enabling simulations with over $10^{11}$ Pauli strings and extending the accessible timescales of quantum many-body dynamics.

We report the entanglement-based segment of the Slovak national quantum communication infrastructure, connecting four cities in western Slovakia over deployed telecommunication fiber. Abstract: Memory effects in open quantum systems do not by themselves imply quantum memory, since non-Markovian dynamics can often be reproduced by classical-memory models. Known criteria certify the presence of quantum memory in the environment by comparing the evolution of the system at two different times. We derive an analytical three-time witness and use it to certify quantum memory in a minimal qubit example in which every pair of times has a classical-memory explanation, thus establishing a strict hierarchy of quantum memory effects. Genuine multi-time quantum memory does not rely on fine-tuned time-dependent Hamiltonians or gate-based evolution. We experimentally demonstrate the hierarchy of quantum memory in a time-continuous realization on a superconducting-qubit platform with time-independent couplings.

Abstract: We investigate boundary-induced stiffness in discrete-time quantum walks confined to a finite lattice by unitary reflecting boundaries implemented through coin-flip operations. We define a dimensionless spreading stiffness from the instantaneous variation of the spatial width relative to the asymptotic spreading rate of the corresponding unconfined Hadamard walk and compare localized, Gaussian, and truncated-uniform initial states. The long-time response retains a strong dependence on the initial spatial profile: the localized and truncated-uniform states show an overall increase in stiffness with increasing confinement size, whereas the Gaussian state exhibits a nonmonotonic dependence with a broad maximum at intermediate sizes. The first and second stiffness moments satisfy the expected asymptotic consistency relation in the high-stiffness regime. The normalized Kullback--Leibler divergence reveals a slower spatial reorganization and qualitatively different finite-size trends, showing that dynamical stiffness and spatial homogenization are distinct properties. These results indicate that unitary confinement produces an intrinsically nonstationary regime whose long-time properties emerge from the interplay between boundary reflections, system size, and the spatial structure of the initial state.

Abstract: Fixed measurements for multiparameter pure-state metrology must remain informative without knowing the input state. We quantify this requirement by the Fisher floor, the smallest fraction of the quantum Fisher information that a measurement retains over all pure states and tangent directions. We determine the Fisher guarantees, namely lower bounds on Fisher floors, implied by projective design order alone in every dimension $d\ge 2$. These guarantees are sharp at order $t=2$ and every odd order $t\ge 3$, and finite weighted designs attain them; at even orders, our bounds are nearly tight. As the order increases, the guarantees approach one half, the single-copy quantum limit. Sampling and spectral completion of any 3-design yield, with probability $1-O(1/d)$, a measurement with linearly many outcomes and a dimension-independent Fisher floor. Such a measurement separates all pure states in Hellinger distance, so pure-state maximum-likelihood estimation from $N$ copies attains mean infidelity $O(d/N)$ uniformly over pure states, optimal up to a universal constant factor.

Abstract: Particle creation in time-dependent quantum fields is a central phenomenon in early-universe cosmology, yet it remains experimentally inaccessible in its original context. We show theoretically that superconducting circuits based on a waveguide-like transmission line loaded with dc-SQUIDs can provide a direct analogue of a scalar field in a radiation-dominated Friedmann--Robertson--Walker universe in conformal time. The mode equation of the circuit reduces to the Weber equation, the same equation governing cosmological particle creation in a radiation-dominated background, admitting an exact solution in terms of parabolic cylinder functions and an exponentially suppressed particle spectrum. However, the requirement that the condensate acquires a flux-dependent macroscopic CM momentum persists. This ensures that the critical temperature $T_c$ maintains an algebraic LP modulation driven predominantly by the macroscopic kinetic energy of the supercurrent. We bound our theoretical approach by discussing the breakdown of mean-field GL validity before phase fluctuations dominate near the strong-coupling limit.

Abstract: Motivated by superconductors discovered in systems with a strong Coulomb repulsion, we ask whether such a repulsion can give rise to superconductivity with a transition temperature $T_c$ bounded only by the electron bandwidth. Coulomb repulsion can produce pairing as it binds electrons to holes. If each hole binds three electrons into a three-electron-one-hole (3e1h) composite, the condensation of these bosonic composites can give rise to superconductivity. However, the resulting $T_c$ falls far below the electron bandwidth in the controlled regime where the binding is much stronger than the carriers' kinetic energy. This is because, on a lattice, a tightly bound composite usually has to move through a higher-order process where the intermediate state costs an energy of order the binding energy, so that the strong binding makes the composite heavy, limiting the superfluid stiffness and hence $T_c$. Here, we show that symmetry offers a way around this tradeoff. In lattices satisfying certain symmetry requirements, a composite can move by single-particle hops without any costly intermediate state, a motion we call rolling. Such rolling keeps the mass of the composite finite even for an infinitely strong binding, so that a $T_c$ proportional to the electron bandwidth can be achieved in the controlled regime. We derive a criterion for rolling and apply it to construct all fourteen minimal lattice models that support such rolling. We show that rollable 3e1h composites can indeed be stabilized in the ground state. We then propose to realize such superconductivity in a dual-gate bilayer, and use exact numerics to show that $T_c$ can reach a few percent of the electron hopping. These results establish rollability as a guiding principle in the search for and design of superconductors in strongly repelling electron systems, with the minimal models constructed here as prototypes.

</details>

Abstract: Superconductors expel magnetic fields through the Meissner effect. Even slightly above the superconducting transition temperature, electrons can briefly form Cooper pairs, leading to an orbital diamagnetic susceptibility that diverges as the transition is approached. In this work, we use Gaussian fluctuation theory to study a generic multicomponent superconductor above its transition temperature and show that fluctuations can instead produce a paramagnetic response. We identify two competing contributions: the usual diamagnetic contribution from the center-of-mass motion of Cooper pairs and a paramagnetic contribution from interference between different components of the superconducting order parameter. The latter is related to pair quantum geometry, which describes how the internal components of the Cooper pair change with momentum. We then apply our theory to a model of a kagome superconductor with two chiral pairing modes. When the parent state preserves time-reversal symmetry, the paramagnetic contribution can outweigh the conventional diamagnetic contribution, producing net fluctuation paramagnetism. However, when the orbital time-reversal symmetry is broken, e.g., by forming a weak loop-current order, the degeneracy between the two chiral modes is then lifted. Upon cooling toward the superconducting transition, the lower-energy mode dominates the fluctuations, and diamagnetism wins as single-component physics takes over. Our results provide a mechanism for fluctuation paramagnetism in superconductors and show that the orbital magnetic response can probe the quantum geometry of Cooper pairs.

Abstract: $O(N)$ models provide a versatile framework for describing continuous phase transitions in a wide variety of physical systems. We investigate an extension of these models arising from order parameters that transform under interlocked internal and spatial symmetries which describe transitions towards non-polar structural order. Such order occurs, for instance, in dipolar antiferromagnets, spin-orbit-coupled magnets, non-polar ferroics, and certain nematic systems. The resulting effective theory is an $O(d)$ model with an additional symmetry-allowed kinetic term $h(\partial_iφ_i)^2$, which modifies the relative stiffness of longitudinal and transverse fluctuations. We study both thermal and quantum phase transitions within this $O(d)$ model using the functional renormalization group (FRG). In contrast to conventional $O(N)$ models, the interlocking of internal and spatial symmetries distinguishes spatial and imaginary-time derivatives, such that the quantum model is not simply obtained from the thermal one by a dimensional extension $d\rightarrow d+1$. We derive and analyze the corresponding FRG flow equations, paying particular attention to the tensor structure of the two-point function, which is essential for consistently extracting the flow of the kinetic terms. Our analysis shows that the additional kinetic coupling is irrelevant at the $O(d)$ Wilson-Fisher fixed point in both thermal and quantum cases, where quantum-to-classical correspondence thus re-emerges. However, its small but sizable correction-to-scaling exponent gives rise to substantial corrections to scaling in the vicinity of the critical point.

</details>

Abstract: Landau quantization can induce nematic order in a two-dimensional Fermi liquid that is isotropic at zero magnetic field. Using a mean-field theory near a quadrupolar Pomeranchuk instability, we show that varying the magnetic field produces alternating nematic and isotropic regions, with transitions that can be either continuous or first order. The resulting nematic state also leaves distinctive signatures in the de Haas--van Alphen oscillations. These contrasting behaviors provide experimentally relevant signatures of field-induced nematicity and reveal the importance of thermodynamic constraints in quantum-oscillation measurements.

Abstract: Quantum oscillations are widely used to probe the Fermi surface of metals, but their behavior becomes more complex when electronic interactions can spontaneously deform that surface. We investigate this interplay in a two-dimensional model of interacting electrons capable of developing nematic order, using a uniform mean-field approximation in a perpendicular magnetic field. We show that Landau quantization can favor or suppress nematic order as the field varies, producing alternating isotropic and nematic regions near a Pomeranchuk instability, with both continuous and first-order transitions. Conversely, the evolving nematic order modifies the de Haas--van Alphen oscillations in ways that depend on the thermodynamic conditions. At fixed chemical potential, the oscillation frequency can change significantly even when the cyclotron mass varies only modestly. At fixed particle density, conservation of the Fermi-surface area preserves the leading Onsager frequency, while the cyclotron mass and harmonic amplitudes remain sensitive to nematic order. Abstract: We develop a framework to study subsystem symmetry-protected topological (SSPT) phases using lattice response theory. We begin with a systematic construction of spacetime lattice gauge fields from general microscopic $X$-type symmetry actions on qudit systems, including global symmetries, subsystem symmetries, and more general modulated symmetries. Given a gauge-invariant response action, we then construct a corresponding entangler and explicitly show that it implements stacking with the SSPT phase described by the response action. Abstract: Light can manipulate magnetic order and its excitations on ultrafast timescales, including through resonantly driven phonons. In antiferromagnets, light can also create correlated magnon pairs, or bimagnons. Unlike single magnons, whose damping has been measured and modeled in detail, little is known about the lifetime, interactions and decay of such correlated pairs: prior two-magnon measurements show broad linewidths or coherences that dephase within 200 fs. Here we pump the phonon-assisted two-magnon absorption of NiO with mid-infrared pulses, far below its bandgap, and probe the resulting magnetic dynamics at finite momentum with time-resolved resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS). Upon photoexcitation, the one-magnon linewidth and energy are essentially unchanged, indicating that the long-range magnetic order is preserved without the photocarrier-induced demagnetization characteristic of above-bandgap excitation. A pump-induced two-magnon signal appears on the energy-gain (anti-Stokes) side of the spectrum. Its spectral weight peaks 4 ps after the pump, well beyond our 0.5 ps time resolution, indicative of a delayed growth likely due to momentum redistribution of the light-induced magnon pairs across the Brillouin zone. This two-magnon signal decays with a time constant of $28\pm11$ ps, exceeding the previously reported two-magnon dephasing time by more than two orders of magnitude. These results establish long-lived light-driven bimagnon correlations beyond the one-magnon state, akin to the higher-order correlations measured in ultracold gases.
